height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}}
. The component of
\tilde{\kappa}
in the

representation does not contribute to the charge
Q[\kappa]
as
Z[\kappa]
in eq. (
49
) vanishes identically when
\tilde{\kappa}
is fully antisymmetric. Therefore, only those
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensors in the irreducible
{[4,1]}
representation of
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
contribute to non-zero charges, so we will restrict
\tilde{\kappa}
to be a
{[4,1]}
-tensor.

4
\kappa
-charge as a special case of
\Omega[V]

4.1
Charges for Minkowski space

The Penrose currents
Y[K]
, which have been shown to give a gauge-invariant construction of the ADM and
\lambda
-charges, are not related to the
\kappa
-charges. Indeed, as we have stressed,
Y[K]
is not the most general covariant conserved 2-form. Instead, the most general 2-form which is linear in the linearised Riemann tensor takes the form

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}

(50)

where
V
is a
{\lfloor 2,2,2\rfloor}
-tensor which must satisfy particular constraints in order for
\Omega[V]
to be conserved.
Each
V
satisfying these constraints gives a co-closed 2-form which can be integrated to give a conserved charge

\mathbb{Q}[V]=\int_{\Sigma_{d-2}}\star\Omega[V]

(51)

where,
\Sigma_{d-2}
is a codimension-2 cycle.
If
\Omega[V]
is co-exact, then
\mathbb{Q}[V]=0
on any cycle
\Sigma_{d-2}
and there is no corresponding 1-form symmetry.

We have seen in section
2.3
that
\Omega[V]
reduces to the Penrose 2-form
Y[K]
when
V
is given by eq. (
38
), which then provides a covariantisation of the ADM and dual ADM charges in eq. (
35
). We now ask if there is a choice of
V
for which
\Omega[V]
agrees with the secondary current
J[\kappa]
introduced in eq. (
45
), subject to the gauge choice (
44
).
If such a construction were possible, then the charge
\mathbb{Q}[V]
would provide a gauge-invariant formulation of the
\kappa
-charges.

Given a
\kappa
-tensor, suppose there exists a
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
satisfying

\partial^{\mu}V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}\tilde{\kappa}_{\nu\alpha\beta[\gamma|\delta]}

(52)

Then, for such a
V
, we find

\displaystyle\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=J[\kappa]_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{\alpha}\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}

(53)

where

\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}=2\Gamma^{\gamma\delta|\beta}V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}

(54)

This implies that
\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]
is conserved,

\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]_{\mu\nu}=0

(55)

from eq. (
48
), so that

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]\equiv\int_{\Sigma_{d-2}}\star\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]

(56)

is a
gauge-invariant
conserved charge constructed from the
\kappa
tensor.
Eq. (
53
) gives an explicit relation between the secondary currents
J[\kappa]
associated with the
\kappa
-invariances of the dual graviton and the covariant 2-forms
\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]
so that the charges are related by

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=Q[\kappa]+\int_{\Sigma_{d-2}}\differential\star\Phi

(57)

Using eq. (
49
), the covariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is a total derivative

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int_{\Sigma_{d-2}}\differential\star(Z[\kappa]+\Phi)

(58)

and so is a topological charge
which will only be non-zero if
h_{\mu\nu}
is not
globally defined.

The covariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is then the sum of
Q[\kappa]=\int\differential\star Z[\kappa]
and a further total derivative term
\int\differential\star\Phi
.
Neither
Z[\kappa]
nor
\Phi
is gauge-invariant, but their sum gives the gauge-invariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int\differential\star(Z[\kappa]+\Phi)
.
In section
6
we will consider examples where the charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is non-zero and will find a choice of gauge where the
\Phi
term does not contribute, giving
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=Q[\kappa]
. In other gauges, both terms contribute to
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
for these examples.
However, in general the
\Phi
term need not vanish with the gauge condition (
44
), so in general the two charges
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
and
Q[\kappa]
differ, even with the gauge choice (
44
).
Both are conserved topological charges given by the integral of a total derivative, with
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
a gauge-invariant charge and
Q[\kappa]
a non-gauge-invariant Noether-type charge associated with a gauge invariance of the dual theory.
Recall that for branes, both covariant and non-covariant charges play a role and are related but distinct
[
26
]
.

We can gain some further insight by analysing these charges in the dual formulation of the theory. There,
Q[\kappa]
is an electric charge for the dual graviton theory, while the covariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
can be understood as a covariant charge for the dual graviton. Neither
Q[\kappa]
nor
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is a topological charge for the dual graviton theory, but their difference is the integral of a total derivative that will vanish for solutions for which the dual graviton is globally defined, but can give a non-zero topological charge more generally. Adding this topological term to
Q[\kappa]
then covariantises it to give the gauge-invariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
.
We will return to this observation in an upcoming work.

This discussion hinges on the existence of a
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
satisfying eq. (
52
) for a given
\kappa
-tensor. If the background spacetime is Minkowski space, possibly with some points or regions removed but
without
any compactified directions, a solution is easily found to be

V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|[\gamma}x_{\delta]}

(59)

It is straightforward to verify that this satisfies eq. (
52
) for any
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensor by using eq. (
43
). This choice of
V
is clearly not unique, and any
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V_{0}
satisfying
\partial^{\mu}(V_{0})_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0
can be added to
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
to define another valid solution. At this point, we merely point out that a choice of
V
is available which relates the charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
and
Q[\kappa]
on Minkowski space.
In section
7.1
we will discuss in more generality the charges
\mathbb{Q}[V_{0}]
which can arise from the inclusion of such extra terms in eq. (
59
).

In the more general discussion of section
7
, we will see that it is sufficient to require
V
to be an irreducible
{[4,2]}
-tensor (rather than an arbitrary
{\lfloor 2,2,2\rfloor}
-tensor) in order to construct all the distinct charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
. Therefore, only the irreducible
{[4,2]}
part of
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
given in eq. (
59
) will contribute to the charges. Note that if
V
is irreducible, then eq. (
52
) implies that
\tilde{\kappa}
is an irreducible
{[4,1]}
-tensor. This is not a restriction as only irreducible
\tilde{\kappa}
tensors give non-zero charges (see section
3
).

4.2
Choice of
V
for solutions with compact directions

The situation is more complicated when the background spacetime has compact directions.
If
\mathcal{M}
is the product of
T^{n}
with
(d-n)
-dimensional Minkowski space (or Minkowski space with some regions removed) then
V
must be periodic in the torus coordinates
y^{i}
to be well-defined. In this work, we will achieve this by requiring that
\partial_{y^{i}}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0
. We now discuss the explicit constructions which give solutions of eq. (
52
) on such spaces.

Let us begin with the simplest case of
d=5
and
n=1
, so
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1}
. We denote the coordinates by
x^{\mu}=(x^{m},y)
.
Crucially, the definition (
59
) is not well-defined on this space since the component

V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|my}=-\frac{1}{4}(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}y-\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|y}x_{m})

(60)

is not periodic in
y
.
Indeed, for a general choice of
\tilde{\kappa}
on a background spacetime which has compact directions, eq. (
52
) may not have a well-defined solution and the manipulations leading to eq. (
53
) may break down.

However, for the five-dimensional case, there are two possible solutions to this apparent problem which can arise for specific choices of
\tilde{\kappa}
. The first possibility is the simple observation that
V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|my}
in eq. (
60
) above
is
well-defined if the components
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}=0
. That is, for particular choices of
\tilde{\kappa}
, constructing
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
as in eq. (
59
) remains valid.
The second possibility is that, while
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
in eq. (
59
) may be ill-defined for a given
\tilde{\kappa}
, there may exist another
{[4,2]}
-tensor
V
which solves eq. (
52
). Then the manipulations leading to the crucial result in eq. (
53
) go through as before. The expression given in eq. (
59
) is merely one possible solution on Minkowski space.
To summarise, for a given solution there may not be a
V
satisfying (
52
) for general
\kappa
, so we seek those
\kappa
for which there is such a
V
and then use that
V[\kappa]
in our construction.

Let us study a pair of cases that are relevant to the examples we consider later. In
d=5
, the irreducible
\kappa
tensors are in the

representation.
For the example studied in section
6.1
, the
\kappa
-tensor that gives a non-zero charge has non-vanishing components

\kappa_{y|y}=\kappa_{0}

(61)

where
\kappa_{0}
is a constant.
The dual (
41
) of this tensor has non-vanishing components

\tilde{\kappa}_{tabc|y}=\kappa_{0}\epsilon_{abc}

(62)

Note that, in particular, the
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}
components all vanish and so
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
given by eq. (
59
) is well-defined. This is a realisation of the first solution outlined above.

For the charge studied in section
6.2
, the relevant
\kappa
-tensor has non-zero components

\kappa_{t|y}=\kappa_{y|t}=\kappa_{0}

(63)

The dual of this tensor has

\tilde{\kappa}_{abct|t}=\tilde{\kappa}_{abcy|y}=-\epsilon_{abc}\kappa_{0}

(64)

and all other components of
\tilde{\kappa}
which are not related to these by anti-symmetry vanish.
In this case,
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
defined in eq. (
59
) is not well-defined on the compact direction and we must seek a different, well-defined
V
which satisfies eq. (
52
) for this
\tilde{\kappa}
.
We consider instead the
{[4,2]}
-tensor
V
with components

V_{abct|dt}=V_{abcy|dy}=-\frac{1}{8}\kappa_{0}\epsilon_{abc}x_{d}

(65)

and all other components not related to these by anti-symmetry vanish. It can be straightforwardly checked that this solves eq. (
52
) for
\tilde{\kappa}
given in eq. (
64
). Furthermore, it has no dependence on the
S^{1}
coordinate
y
, so is well-defined on
\mathcal{M}
.
It is a
{[4,2]}
-tensor as
\epsilon_{[abc}x_{d]}=0
with
a,b=1,2,3
. This is a realisation of the second solution described above.

We now consider solutions in
d>5
dimensions. In particular, we fix
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times T^{n}
with
n>1
. Cases with Minkowski space of greater than four dimensions can be analysed in an analogous manner. With
n>1
,
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
in eq. (
59
) has components

\displaystyle V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{2(d-1)}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}x_{n}-\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|n}x_{m}\right)

(66)

\displaystyle V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|mi}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{2(d-1)}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}y_{i}-\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|i}x_{m}\right)

(67)

\displaystyle V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{2(d-1)}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|i}y_{j}-\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|j}y_{i}\right)

(68)

In order for the
V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|mi}
components to be well-defined on the toroidal directions, we require
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}=0
for all
m
. For the
V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|ij}
components to be well-defined, we require
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|i}=0
. Therefore, unless
\tilde{\kappa}=0
(and so
V[\tilde{\kappa}]=0
), this choice of
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
is not well-defined. Therefore, we are forced to find an alternative solution to eq. (
52
).

We now describe such solutions for the
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensors given above in eqs. (
62
) and (
64
).
In
d>5
dimensions, irreducible
\kappa
-tensors are in the
{[d-4,1]}
representation. The
\kappa
-tensor whose dual is the
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensor in eq. (
62
) has components

\kappa_{y_{1}y_{2}\dots y_{d-4}|y_{1}}=\kappa_{0}

(69)

in
d
-dimensions. Let us define a
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V
with components

V_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|mi}=V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|mi}

(70)

with the right-hand side given in eq. (
67
) and all other components vanishing. Since
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma|m}=0
for this example, this
V
is well-defined. It is straightforward to verify that it satisfies eq. (
52
) for
\tilde{\kappa}
in eq. (
62
).

Finally, let us the consider the
d>5
dimensional case with
\tilde{\kappa}
given by eq. (
64
). The
\kappa
-tensor dual to this is

\kappa_{ty_{2}\dots y_{d-4}|y_{1}}=\kappa_{y_{1}y_{2}\dots y_{d-4}|t}=\kappa_{0}

(71)

The tensor
V
constructed in eq. (
65
) remains well-defined and satisfies eq. (
52
) for this
\tilde{\kappa}
.

4.3
Condition for non-trivial charge

While a conserved current
\Omega[V]
can be built for each
{\lfloor 4,1\rfloor}
-tensor
\tilde{\kappa}
by using
V[\kappa]
in eq. (
59
), we note that if
\tilde{\kappa}
is of the form

\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma}=\partial^{\sigma}\rho_{\sigma\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma}

(72)

for some
{\lfloor 5,1\rfloor}
-tensor
\rho
, then the charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
vanishes. This follows as

\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]_{\mu\nu}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\partial^{\sigma}\rho_{\sigma\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma}x_{\delta}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}\partial^{\sigma}(R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\rho_{\sigma\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma}x_{\delta})\equiv\partial^{\sigma}\Xi_{\mu\nu\sigma}

(73)

where we have used
\partial_{[\sigma}R_{\alpha\beta]\gamma\delta}=0
in the final equality.
\Xi
is a globally defined 3-form since it depends only on the curvature, so
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int\star\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]=\int\differential\star\Xi=0
by Stokes’ theorem as the integral is assumed to be taken over a cycle. Therefore, non-trivial
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
charges only occur when the spacetime admits
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensors satisfying eq. (
43
) which are not of the form (
72
). This implies a notion of cohomology which we will develop in the following sections.

5
Charges and cohomology

We have seen in section
4.3
that if
\kappa
is
\differential_{L}
-exact, then the charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
vanishes. Indeed, the same is true of the non-covariant charge
Q[\kappa]

[
2
]
. Hence, in order to construct non-zero charges we require a
\kappa
-tensor in a non-trivial
\differential_{L}
-cohomology class.

Furthermore, in order for the charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int\star\Omega[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]
to be non-zero, the
(d-2)
-form
\star\Omega[V]
must itself
be closed and not exact, so that it
represents a non-trivial de Rham cohomology class. There are, therefore, two different forms which must be in non-trivial cohomology classes for there to be a non-zero charge. We consider them in turn below.

5.1
\differential_{L}
-cohomology for
\kappa
-tensors

We now consider the general form for the
\kappa
-tensors, following
[
2
]
.
The
\kappa
-tensors satisfy
eq. (
33
), which we repeat here:

\partial_{[\mu}\kappa_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}]|\beta}=0

(74)

i.e. they are
\differential_{L}
-closed.
As pointed out previously,
\differential_{L}
-exact
\kappa
-tensors will satisfy this automatically. That is, if
\kappa_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}=\partial_{[\alpha_{1}}\rho_{\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{d-4}]|\beta}
for some
{\lfloor d-5,1\rfloor}
-tensor
\rho
, then eq. (
74
) is trivially satisfied.
4
4

4

In
d=5
this remains true when
\kappa
is reducible, but not when it is irreducible. In that case, the relevant
\differential_{L}
-exact form for a
\kappa
-tensor is
\kappa_{\mu|\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\psi
for a scalar
\psi
.
We also know from section
4.3
that the charges
Q[\kappa]
and
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
vanish in this case, so these charges depend only on the

\differential_{L}
-cohomology class.

We begin with the case of reducible
\kappa
-tensors, which are in the reducible
{\lfloor d-4,1\rfloor}
representation. This is a tensor product representation of the fully antisymmetric
(d-4)
-form representation and the 1-form representation.
As such, reducible
\kappa
-tensors can be written as a linear combination of tensors of the basic type

\kappa^{\text{red.}}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}=\tau_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}}\sigma_{\beta}

(75)

where
\tau
is a
(d-4)
-form and
\sigma
is a 1-form.
For such a tensor, eq. (
74
) is satisfied if
\tau
is closed and
\sigma
is constant, and will not be
\differential_{L}
-exact only if
\tau
is not exact.
Then the non-trivial
\kappa
-tensors are constructed from
(d-4)
-forms

\tau
that represent a non-trivial cohomology class, together with constant 1-forms. Requiring non-trivial
\differential_{L}
-cohomology for
\kappa
then relies on having a non-trivial de Rham cohomology group
H^{d-4}(\mathcal{M})
.
General solutions then arise from taking linear combinations of terms of the form (
75
).
An alternative way of seeing this is to think of
\kappa_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}
as being a vector-valued
(d-4)
-form, which is closed and non-exact for each value of
\beta
.

If we instead consider irreducible
\kappa
-tensors, for which

\kappa^{\text{irred.}}_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta]}=0

(76)

these can be written as a linear combination of tensors of the form

\kappa^{\text{irred.}}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}=\tau_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}}\sigma_{\beta}-\tau_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}}\sigma_{\beta]}

(77)

This follows from the fact that all irreducible
{[p,q]}
-tensors can be written as the Young symmetrised version of a reducible
{\lfloor p,q\rfloor}
-tensor, and because the Young symmetriser is a linear operator. Indeed,
\kappa^{\text{irred.}}
is simply the Young symmetrised version of
\kappa^{\text{red.}}
onto the irreducible
{[d-4,1]}

GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representation.

For closed
\tau
and constant
\sigma
, we have

\partial_{[\mu}\kappa^{\text{irred.}}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}]|\beta}=(-1)^{d-3}\frac{d-4}{d-3}\partial_{[\mu|}\tau_{\beta|\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-5}}\sigma_{\alpha_{d-4}]}

(78)

Demanding that this vanishes implies

\partial_{\beta}(\tau_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}}\sigma_{\mu]})=0

(79)

so
\tau\wedge\sigma=\text{constant}
. This is solved by

\tau=\tau_{0}+\beta\wedge\sigma

(80)

where
\tau_{0}
is a constant
(d-4)
-form and
\beta
is a closed
(d-5)
-form (such that
\tau
is closed). Substituting back into eq. (
77
), we see that

\kappa^{\text{irred.}}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}=(\kappa_{0})_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}+\beta_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-5}}\sigma_{\alpha_{d-4}]}\sigma_{\beta}

(81)

where
(\kappa_{0})_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}=(\tau_{0})_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}}\sigma_{\beta}-(\tau_{0})_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}}\sigma_{\beta]}
is a constant irreducible
{[d-4,1]}
tensor.
A general irreducible
\kappa
tensor can be written as a linear combination of tensors of the form of eq. (
81
). Since the constant term
\kappa_{0}
can be written as a linear combination of tensors of the form
\beta_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-5}}\sigma_{\alpha_{d-4}]}\sigma_{\beta}
where
\beta
is taken to be a constant
(d-5)
-form, we can equivalently write the general solution as a linear combination of tensors of the form

\kappa^{\text{irred.}}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-4}|\beta}=\beta_{[\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{d-5}}S_{\alpha_{d-4}]|\beta}

(82)

where
S_{\alpha|\beta}
is a symmetric constant
{[1,1]}
-tensor. Then
\kappa
will be
\differential_{L}
-closed and not
\differential_{L}
-exact if
\beta
is closed and not exact.

Given this knowledge of the
\kappa
tensors, we return to the question of their
\differential_{L}
-cohomology. In order to construct non-trivial charges
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
there must exist a
\differential_{L}
-closed
\kappa
tensor which is not
\differential_{L}
-exact. From the considerations above, it is clear that this is only possible if the de Rham cohomology group
H^{d-4}(\mathcal{M})
is non-trivial in the case of reducible
\kappa
tensors, and
H^{d-5}(\mathcal{M})
is non-trivial for irreducible ones.

Let us consider the
\kappa
-tensors given in section
4.2
, which will be used in the examples studied in section
6
. The background spacetime is
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1}
with
y
the coordinate on the
S^{1}
. Firstly, consider the five-dimensional
\kappa
-tensor in eq. (
61
) with
\kappa_{y|y}=\kappa_{0}
. Let us first treat this as an irreducible tensor. If the
y
coordinate were non-compact, then this would be trivial since
\kappa_{\mu|\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\psi
for
\psi=\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{0}y^{2}
. However, for
y
periodic,
\psi=\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{0}y^{2}
is not a well-defined 0-form and so in this case
\kappa
is
d_{L}
-closed but not
d_{L}
-exact. If we treat this
\kappa
as a reducible tensor instead, we could write
\kappa_{\mu|\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}
with
\xi_{y}=\kappa_{0}y
if
y
were a non-compact coordinate. Again, when
y
is periodic this is not a well-defined 1-form and the
\kappa
-tensor is non-trivial.

The other example of interest uses the
\kappa
-tensor (
63
), with components
\kappa_{y|t}=\kappa_{t|y}=\kappa_{0}
. Again, if
y
were non-compact we could write
\kappa_{\mu|\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\psi
with
\psi=\kappa_{0}ty
. However, with
y
periodic this is not well-defined. Therefore, this
\kappa
-tensor is
\differential_{L}
-closed and not
\differential_{L}
-exact. Treating
\kappa
as a reducible
{\lfloor 1,1\rfloor}
-tensor, we could write
\kappa_{\mu|\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}
where
\xi
has components
\xi_{t}=\kappa_{0}y
and
\xi_{y}=\kappa_{0}t
. Again, this is not well-defined when
y
is periodic, so the
\kappa
-tensor is not
\differential_{L}
-exact. Similarly, the higher-dimensional
\kappa
-tensors in eqs. (
69
) and (
71
) are
\differential_{L}
-closed and not
\differential_{L}
-exact when there are compact directions.

In this work we study the graviton theory on Minkowski space with points or regions removed, and possibly with directions toroidally compactified. Let us first consider the case without toroidal directions.
Minkowski space has trivial cohomology, so non-trivial charges can only arise if some region is excised from Minkowski space.
A non-trivial
H^{d-4}(\mathcal{M})
can be generated in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
by excising a 2-brane (with 3-dimensional worldvolume) and so considering
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{2})
then
H^{d-4}(\mathcal{M})=H^{d-4}(\mathbb{R}^{d-3}\setminus\{\text{point}\})\cong\mathbb{R}
. A closed but non-exact
(d-4)
-form in
\mathcal{M}
is given, for example, by the volume form of a
(d-4)
-sphere wrapping the 2-brane.
Similarly, excising a 3-brane (with 4-dimensional worldvolume) gives non-trivial
H^{d-5}(\mathcal{M})
.

Consider now the case of a toroidal compactification.
Then
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{1,n}\times T^{m}
has non-trivial
H^{d-4}(\mathcal{M})
provided
n\leq 3
and has non-trivial
H^{d-5}(\mathcal{M})
provided
n\leq 4
. Obviously, points and/or regions could also be removed from these compactified spaces to generate further non-trivial cohomology groups.

5.2
Cohomology for
\Omega[V]

To obtain a non-zero charge, the
(d-2)
-form
\star\Omega[V]
should be in non-trivial cohomology class, so
H^{d-2}(\mathcal{M})

should be non-trivial.
In the examples we consider in the next section
\mathcal{M}=(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\text{point}\})\times\mathbb{R}\times T^{d-4}
, i.e. the product of
a spatial
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with a point removed
with a
(d-4)
-torus and a time-like line.
The
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with a point removed has a non-trivial 2-cycle given by a 2-sphere surrounding the point and taking the product of this with
T^{d-4}
gives a non-trivial cycle
\Sigma_{d-2}
of codimension 2 that can be integrated over.
This space also has non-trivial
H^{d-5}(\mathcal{M})
and
H^{d-4}(\mathcal{M})
represented by forms on the torus and these can be used in constructing
\kappa
.

6
Some solutions and their charges

Ref.
[
2
]
discussed various solutions of the linearised theory which carry the charges
Q[k]
,
Q[\kappa]
and
Q[\lambda]
and the covariant Penrose charges of these were calculated in
[
6
]
. Here, we focus on the charges
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
, related to the non-covariant charges
Q[\kappa]
by eq. (
56
), and study solutions which carry these. We first analyse a solution of the linearised theory derived from the Kaluza-Klein monopole solution and then one obtained from Lorentzian Taub-NUT space.

6.1
Gravitational monopoles in
d\geq 5

The linearisation of the Kaluza-Klein monopole solution considered in
[
2
]
is a linear superposition of a solution with mass and a solution which carries a magnetic charge. Here we focus on the latter part of the solution, which is in itself a valid solution of the linearised theory. (The other part has

Q[\kappa]=0
and
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=0
.)

We first consider the linearised Kaluza-Klein Ansatz on the space
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1}
with the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
at
\vec{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
removed; we denote this space
(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1})^{\bullet}
.
The coordinates will be taken to be
(t,x^{a},y)
where
y\sim y+2\pi R_{y}
. The metric on the space is
\eta=(-1,1,1,1,1)
. We take the graviton Ansatz

h_{ay}=2A_{a}(x^{b})

(83)

where the field strength
F=\differential{A}
is given by

F_{ab}=\epsilon_{abc}\partial_{c}\widetilde{W}

(84)

and
\widetilde{W}
is given by

\widetilde{W}(x^{a})=-\frac{p}{\absolutevalue{x}}

(85)

where
p
is a constant parameterising the monopole charge. The non-vanishing connection coefficients are

\Gamma_{ab|y}=F_{ab},\quad\Gamma_{ay|b}=\partial_{a}A_{b}

(86)

and the non-vanishing components of the linearised Riemann tensor are

R_{abcy}=\partial_{c}F_{ab}

(87)

It has been shown in Ref.
[
2
]
that this solution has a non-vanishing charge
Q[\kappa]
corresponding to a
\kappa
-tensor with non-zero components

\kappa_{y|y}=\kappa_{0}

(88)

in five dimensions, with
\kappa_{0}
a constant. As discussed in section
5
, this is a
\differential_{L}
-closed but not
\differential_{L}
-exact
{[1,1]}
-tensor on
(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1})^{\bullet}
. The charge is defined over a codimension-2 surface
\Sigma_{3}=S^{2}\times S^{1}
where
S^{2}
is a 2-sphere containing the origin within a constant time hypersurface of
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
, and
S^{1}
is the circle parameterised by
y
, for which eq. (
47
) yields

Q[\kappa]=8\pi^{2}R_{y}\kappa_{0}p

(89)

We now evaluate the covariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
given in eq. (
56
) for this solution. First note that the Hodge dual
\tilde{\kappa}=\star_{L}\kappa
of the
\kappa
-tensor in eq. (
88
), given by eq. (
41
), has non-vanishing components

\tilde{\kappa}_{tabc|y}=\kappa_{0}\epsilon_{abc}

(90)

This
\kappa
-tensor was discussed in section
4.2
, where we showed that the
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
in eq. (
59
) is well-defined in this case.
For the
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensor in eq. (
90
),
V
has non-vanishing components

V_{tabc|y\alpha}=-\frac{1}{8}\kappa_{0}\epsilon_{abc}x_{\alpha}

(91)

where
x^{\alpha}=(t,x^{a})
, which is well-defined on
(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1})^{\bullet}
.
Explicit calculation then yields

\Omega[V]_{at}=p\kappa_{0}\frac{x_{a}}{\absolutevalue{x}^{3}}

(92)

The charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is defined over a codimension-2 surface which we again take as
\Sigma_{3}=S^{2}\times S^{1}
. The integration yields

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\star\Omega[V]=16\pi^{2}R_{y}p\kappa_{0}

(93)

Note that this is a factor of 2 larger than
Q[\kappa]
in eq. (
89
). The difference comes from the final term in eq. (
56
). This solution has a non-globally defined
h
and so
\int\differential\star\Phi
, i.e. the final term in eq. (
56
), can be non-zero. Indeed, explicit evaluation gives

\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\differential\star\Phi=8\pi^{2}R_{y}\kappa_{0}p

(94)

so that eqs. (
89
) and (
94
) contribute equally and sum to give
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
in eq. (
93
), as expected from the general result in eq. (
56
).

As described in section
4
, while
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is gauge-invariant, neither of the two terms on the right-hand side of eq. (
56
) (that is,
Q[\kappa]
and
\int\differential\star\Phi
) are gauge-invariant. In the gauge presented above (i.e. using the graviton components (
83
)), the two contributions to
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
given in eqs. (
89
) and (
94
) are equal. In another gauge the two individual contributions will change, with only their sum remaining invariant.

We now ask if it is possible to find a gauge choice where
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=Q[\kappa]
and
\int\differential\star\Phi=0
for this solution, such that
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
gives a gauge-invariant quantity which matches
Q[\kappa]
in this gauge. As indicated in section
4
, we will see that such a gauge choice does exist.
Consider a gauge transformation of the graviton field
h_{\mu\nu}
in eq. (
83
) to

h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}

(95)

where the non-zero components of
\xi
are taken to be

\xi_{a}=4yA_{a}

(96)

We note that the gauge parameter
\xi
is not globally-defined. This is, of course, not an issue. The gauge transformations need only be well-defined within each chart. This is akin to a large gauge transformation winding around the fibre direction in Maxwell theory.

The components of the gauge transformed graviton
h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}
are

h^{\prime}_{ay}=4A_{a},\quad h^{\prime}_{ab}=4y\partial_{(a}A_{b)}

(97)

One can then explicitly check that the linearised Riemann tensors of the two gravitons are equal,
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=R^{\prime}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, as was guaranteed. Therefore the charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is invariant under the gauge transformation. However, calculating the contribution from
\int\differential\star\Phi
in this gauge gives a vanishing result. Indeed, an explicit calculation of
Q[\kappa]^{\prime}
(that is, the charge
Q[\kappa]
evaluated for the gauge transformed graviton
h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}
) verifies that, in this gauge,

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=Q[\kappa]^{\prime}

(98)

As discussed in section
4
, the gravitational duality can be imposed at the level of the connection
\Gamma
in the gauge in which
\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.06758pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.06758pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73412pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.81006pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\mu\nu|}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=0
. The transformation (
96
) ensures that
h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}
satisfies this gauge condition. We note that eq. (
98
) does not hold in this gauge when evaluated on arbitrary solutions, however it does hold for this particular solution.

While this solution is a magnetically charged configuration from the perspective of the graviton field, for the dual graviton description it is an electric charge. Therefore, in the dual theory, the contribution
\int\differential\star\Phi
will vanish and eq. (
98
) follows.
This would then imply that
\int\differential\star\Phi
should also vanish for the graviton theory in this gauge, as we have seen directly.

Higher-dimensional solutions.

One can similarly construct the
\kappa
-charges associated with higher-dimensional solutions with a background spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times T^{d-4})^{\bullet}
for
d>5
. Let the coordinates on
T^{d-4}
be
y^{i}\sim y^{i}+2\pi R_{i}
, with
i=1,\dots,d-4
. We take the Ansatz (
83
) but with
y
taken to be
y^{1}
.
In
d
dimensions,
\kappa
is a
{\lfloor d-4,1\rfloor}
-tensor. We take its non-zero components to be

\kappa_{y_{1}y_{2}\dots y_{d-4}|y_{1}}=\kappa_{0}

(99)

This is proportional to the volume form of
T^{d-4}
.
This is of the form (
81
) where
\beta
is the volume form on the
T^{d-5}\subset T^{d-4}
spanned by
y_{2},\dots,y_{d-4}
, and
\sigma=dy^{1}
.
This
\kappa
is
\differential_{L}
-closed and not
\differential_{L}
-exact. Taking its
\star_{L}
Hodge dual leads to the same components as in eq. (
90
).
As discussed in section
4.2
, in
d>5
,
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
in eq. (
59
) is not well-defined for this solution. Instead, we use the
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
V
in eq. (
70
). This is well-defined on
\mathcal{M}
and solves eq. (
52
) for the
\kappa
in eq. (
99
).
The codimension-2 integration surface is now taken to be
\Sigma_{d-2}=S^{2}\times T^{d-4}
with the
S^{2}
surrounding the origin of a constant time hypersurface of
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
. The resulting charge is

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=8\pi\kappa_{0}p\,\text{Vol}_{T^{d-4}}

(100)

where
\text{Vol}_{T^{d-4}}=\prod_{i=1}^{d-4}2\pi R_{i}
is the volume of the
(d-4)
-torus. Indeed, this reduces to eq. (
93
) for
d=5
.

6.2
Lorentzian Taub-NUT in
d\geq 5

Another example which carries the charge
Q[\kappa]
is the product of linearised Lorentzian Taub-NUT with a torus. The full Taub-NUT metric is dyonic, but here we consider a linearisation only of the magnetically charged part of the solution. In the linearised theory, this is a valid solution in its own right.
We begin with the case of
d=5
on the background
(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times S^{1})^{\bullet}
as in the previous section. We take a graviton field with components

h_{at}=2A_{a}(x^{b})

(101)

where
A_{a}(x^{b})
satisfies

F_{ab}=\epsilon_{abc}\partial_{c}X

(102)

with

X(x^{a})=-\frac{N}{\absolutevalue{x}}

(103)

where
N
is a constant that quantifies the NUT charge of the field configuration.
The non-zero components of the linearised Riemann tensor are

R_{abct}=\partial_{c}F_{ab}

(104)

This example has been studied in Ref.
[
2
]
, where it is shown that it carries the charge

Q[\kappa]=8\pi^{2}R_{y}N\kappa_{0}

(105)

where the
\kappa
-tensor is chosen to be irreducible with non-zero components
\kappa_{t|y}=\kappa_{y|t}\equiv\kappa_{0}=\text{constant}
. (More generally, we could take a reducible
\kappa
-tensor with
\kappa_{t|y}\equiv\kappa_{0}=\text{constant}
and with any
\kappa_{y|t}
and get the same charge.)
The dual is

\tilde{\kappa}_{abct|t}=\tilde{\kappa}_{abcy|y}=-\epsilon_{abc}\kappa_{0}

(106)

and all other components of
\tilde{\kappa}
which are not related to these by anti-symmetry vanish. Indeed, this
\tilde{\kappa}
satisfies eq. (
43
) and is not trivial, as discussed in section
5
.

As we have noted, the form of
V
given in eq. (
59
) is not necessarily well-defined on spacetimes
\mathcal{M}
with toroidal directions, such as the present example.
This example was studied in section
4.2
, where a
{[4,2]}
-tensor
V
was given in eq. (
65
) which is well-defined on
\mathcal{M}
and satisfies eq. (
52
) for this choice of
\tilde{\kappa}
. We repeat the components of
V
here for convenience:

V_{abct|dt}=V_{abcy|dy}=-\frac{1}{8}\kappa_{0}\epsilon_{abc}x_{d}

(107)

All other components not related to these by antisymmetry vanish.

We will evaluate the covariant charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
for this choice of
\kappa
with the integration surface taken to be the codimension-2 cycle
\Sigma_{3}=S^{2}\times S^{1}
where the first factor is a 2-sphere at fixed
r=\absolutevalue{x}
in a constant time slice of
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
and the second factor is the circle parameterised by
y
.
Using eq. (
102
), we find

\Omega[V]_{at}=N\kappa_{0}\frac{x_{a}}{\absolutevalue{x}^{3}}

(108)

Then integrating over
\Sigma_{3}
gives the charge

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\star\Omega[V]=16\pi^{2}R_{y}N\kappa_{0}

(109)

We note that this is a factor of two larger than
Q[\kappa]
in eq. (
105
). Indeed, the remainder is contributed by the
\int\differential\star\Phi
term in eq. (
56
), which evaluates to

\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\differential\star\Phi=8\pi^{2}R_{y}N\kappa_{0}

(110)

as required.

As discussed in the previous example, the fact that the two contributions
Q[\kappa]
and
\int\differential\star\Phi
in eqs. (
105
) and (
110
) are equal is not a gauge-invariant statement.
Similar considerations about gauge dependence to those below eq. (
98
) are true in this case also.
Implementing a gauge transformation
h_{\mu\nu}\to h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}
with gauge parameter
\xi_{a}=4tA_{a}
gives rise to a solution for which
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=Q[\kappa]
and
\int\differential\star\Phi=0
. In this gauge
\Gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.06758pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.06758pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73412pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.81006pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\mu\nu|}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=0
. In the dual graviton description, the Taub-NUT solution carries an electric charge and the
\int\differential\star\Phi
contribution vanishes as this measures a magnetic charge.

Higher-dimensional solutions.

As in the previous sub-section, we now consider higher-dimensional solutions with a background space
(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times T^{d-4})^{\bullet}
for
d>5
. As above, the coordinates on
T^{d-4}
are
y^{i}
with
i=1,\dots,d-4
. Then we can again take the Ansatz (
101
) such that the solution in question is a product of the linearised version of Lorentzian Taub-NUT on
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
with a
(d-4)
-torus. Again, in order to generate a non-trivial charge,
\kappa
is required to be closed but not exact. We take it to have components

\kappa_{ty_{2}\dots y_{d-4}|y_{1}}=\kappa_{y_{1}y_{2}\dots y_{d-4}|t}=\kappa_{0}

(111)

This involves the volume form on
T^{d-5}\subset T^{d-4}
, so is
\differential_{L}
-closed and not
\differential_{L}
-exact. Then evaluating its Hodge dual gives precisely the components in eq. (
106
) as before. The manipulations in eqs. (
107
) and (
108
) are unchanged, but the surface of integration must be codimension-2 in the
d
-dimensional background space so we take it to be
\Sigma_{d-2}=S^{2}\times T^{d-4}\equiv\Sigma_{3}\times T^{d-5}
. Then evaluating the charge on
\Sigma_{d-2}
yields

\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]=\int_{\Sigma_{d-2}}\star\Omega[V]=8\pi N\kappa_{0}\;\text{Vol}_{T^{d-4}}

(112)

This clearly reduces to eq. (
109
) for
d=5
.

7
General construction of covariant conserved charges

In section
4
, we have seen that the 2-forms
\Omega[V]
given in eq. (
50
) include the 2-forms corresponding to both the Penrose and
\kappa
-type charges. We now give a systematic discussion of the constraints that
V
must satisfy in order for
\Omega[V]
to be conserved when
R_{\mu\nu}=0
, and examine the charges that can result. We will find that the non-trivial solutions are in correspondence with the three types of secondary currents mentioned previously,
J[k]
,
J[\lambda]
and
J[\kappa]
, together with identically conserved topological currents.

A priori, the most general gauge-invariant 2-form linear in the Riemann tensor can be written

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}

(113)

for some rank-6 tensor
V
with the symmetries

V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=V_{[\mu\nu]|[\alpha\beta]|[\gamma\delta]}

(114)

that is,
V
is a
{\lfloor 2,2,2\rfloor}
-tensor.
We note that the contraction with the Riemann tensor in eq. (
113
) projects onto the part of
V
which is symmetric under the exchange of the
(\alpha\beta)
and
(\gamma\delta)
indices. We choose not to make this symmetry explicit in our discussion of
V
below as it obscures other anti-symmetries of
V
which we wish to make manifest.
We consider pure gravity in regions where

R_{\alpha\beta}=0

(115)

and demand the conservation of
\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}
,

\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=(\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta})V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}+R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}\partial^{\mu}V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0

(116)

The first term in eq. (
116
) will vanish via the differential Bianchi identity
\partial_{[\mu}R_{\alpha\beta]\gamma\delta}=0
if
V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}
is anti-symmetric in the
\mu
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
indices.
5
5

5

Alternatively,
V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}
could be anti-symmetric in the
\mu
,
\gamma
, and
\delta
indices but, as mentioned above, the contraction with the Riemann tensor imposes that only the part of
V
that is symmetric under interchange of
(\alpha\beta)
with
(\gamma\delta)
contributes to the current, so we choose the former case without loss of generality.
This can be achieved by restricting
V
to be a
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor, i.e.,

V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=V_{[\mu\nu\alpha\beta]|[\gamma\delta]}

(117)

We note that in general
V
does not have to be an
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor in order for
\Omega[V]
to be conserved. Indeed, there are other
{\lfloor 2,2,2\rfloor}
-tensors which can be used to define a conserved
\Omega[V]
. However, we show in appendix
B
that the conserved 2-forms
\Omega[V]
which arise when
V
is such a tensor have a specific form which can also be accounted for by a different choice of
V
which is a
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor. Therefore, as our interest lies in classifying the conserved 2-forms
\Omega[V]
(rather than the tensors
V
themselves) it is sufficient to restrict
V
to be a
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
-tensor.

The
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
representation which
V
transforms under is a tensor product of the fully antisymmetric

and

representations, so is reducible. We also show in appendix
B
that the only irreducible representation in the decomposition of the reducible
{\lfloor 4,2\rfloor}
representation which contributes non-trivially to
\Omega[V]
is the one labelled by the Young tableau

(i.e. the
irreducible

{[4,2]}
representation). Therefore, we can take
V
to be an irreducible
{[4,2]}
-tensor; that is, it must satisfy

V_{[\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma]\delta}=0

(118)

With these facts in hand, the first term in eq. (
116
) vanishes. So the requirement that
\Omega[V]
is conserved reduces to

\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\partial^{\mu}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0

(119)

We now look for constraints on
V
such that
\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=0
when eq. (
115
) is employed (that is, when the field equations
G_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}
are used and sources are absent,
T_{\mu\nu}=0
). We show in Appendix
C
that the most general such constraint is

\partial^{\mu}V^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[11.2791pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.05649pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{|\gamma\delta}}}_{{{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.07574pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33981pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.74245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.74245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.32661pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.94757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[\gamma|\delta]}}}_{{{\nu\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[18.38002pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.38002pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.79797pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.28424pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta]}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.53888pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52776pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{\gamma\delta}_{[\nu\alpha}\theta_{\beta]}

(120)

where
\theta
is a 1-form,
\zeta
is a 3-form and
\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta}
is an irreducible
{[4,1]}
-tensor which is traceless, i.e.
\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[22.13895pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[22.13895pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.2246pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.73184pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=0
.
This constraint is reached from representation theory considerations alone, and the reasoning is as follows. The left-hand side of eq. (
120
) is
\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V
, which is a reducible
{\lfloor 3,2\rfloor}
-tensor and so can be written as a sum of
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representations. These can be branched into
SO(1,d-1)
representations, then eq. (
119
) demands that several representations which appear in the decomposition cannot contribute. In particular, the constraint (
120
) is equivalent to the statement that the only irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations which can contribute to
\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V
are

,

and

. The tensors
\omega

, and
\theta

)). With the more complicated representations involved in the case at hand, such formulae are unwieldy and opaque. Instead, we write expressions in terms of

and

. These objects could be written explicitly in terms of

by evaluating the Young projectors in eqs. (

).
