7.1
Solutions of
\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V=0

We first consider
{[4,2]}
-tensors
V_{0}
for which
\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V_{0}=0
, that is

\partial^{\mu}(V_{0})_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0

(140)

If
V_{0}
is
\differential_{L}
-coexact, then

(V_{0})_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\partial^{\sigma}\hat{V}_{\sigma\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}

(141)

for some
{\lfloor 5,2\rfloor}
-tensor
\hat{V}
, and eq. (
140
) is satisfied. In this case we have

\Omega[V_{0}]_{\mu\nu}=-\partial^{\alpha}\left(R^{\sigma\beta\gamma\delta}\hat{V}_{\sigma\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}\right)

(142)

where the differential Bianchi identity
\partial^{[\alpha}R^{\sigma\beta]\gamma\delta}=0
has been used.
That is,
\Omega[V_{0}]
is the divergence of a globally defined 3-form (since the 3-form depends only on the curvature) so the charge
\mathbb{Q}[V_{0}]=\int\star\Omega[V_{0}]=0
by Stokes’ theorem as the integral is taken over a cycle.

Therefore, non-trivial charges can only arise from
\differential_{L}
-coclosed
{[4,2]}
-tensors (satisfying eq. (
140
)) that are not
\differential_{L}
-coexact (satisfying eq. (
141
)), and the charge depends only on
V_{0}
modulo a
\differential_{L}
-coexact piece.
As a result, the charge corresponds to the
\differential_{L}
-cohomology class of the
{\lfloor d-4,2\rfloor}
-tensor
\star_{L}V_{0}
and this can only be non-zero if the manifold has non-trivial
H^{d-4}
.

If
V_{0}
is
\differential_{L}
-coclosed then there is a locally-defined (i.e. defined in a contractible patch)
{\lfloor 5,2\rfloor}
-tensor
\hat{V}
satisfying (
141
) so that
\Omega[V_{0}]
is a total derivative

\Omega[V_{0}]_{\mu\nu}=\partial^{\rho}W_{\mu\nu\rho}

(143)

where the 3-form
W
can be read off from (
142
).
If
V_{0}
is not
\differential_{L}
-coexact, then
\hat{V}
will not be a globally-defined gauge-invariant tensor.
Recalling the discussion of section
3
, if
W
has non-covariant dependence on the graviton field
h
then this extra term is not co-exact when
h
is non-globally defined. Therefore these terms can produce non-trivial charges for such field configurations.

Alternatively, via similar manipulations to those in eq. (
53
) we find that, for any
V_{0}
satisfying eq. (
140
)

\Omega[V_{0}]_{\mu\nu}=2\partial^{\alpha}\left(\Gamma^{\gamma\delta|\beta}(V_{0})_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}\right)~,

(144)

giving an alternative expression for
W
.

Then given any solution
V
to (
120
), a new solution is obtained by taking
V\to V+V_{0}
where
V_{0}
is any solution of (
140
).
This changes
\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}\to\Omega[V+V_{0}]_{\mu\nu}=\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{\rho}W_{\mu\nu\rho}
so that the current is changed by an identically conserved term
d_{L}^{\dagger}W
. This will change the charge by the integral of a total derivative, and this change will be non-trivial if
W
is not globally-defined.

7.2
Four dimensions

We briefly note that in four dimensions the form of
V
simplifies as it can then be written

V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}v_{\gamma\delta}

(145)

for some 2-form
v
. Now

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}v_{\gamma\delta}=(\star R)_{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}v^{\gamma\delta}

(146)

In regions where
R_{\mu\nu}=0
, the Riemann tensor is equal to the Weyl tensor,
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, so

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=(\star C)_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}v^{\rho\sigma}=C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\star v)^{\rho\sigma}=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}u^{\rho\sigma}

(147)

where we have used
\star C\star=-C
and defined
u=\star v
.
This is of the same form as the Penrose 2-form and is conserved if and only if
u
is a CKY tensor.
Therefore, in
d=4
, the Penrose 2-form is the most general conserved 2-form current.

Indeed, in four dimensions we have seen in section
2
that all the ADM and dual ADM charges of the graviton are already included in the Penrose charges, and the
\kappa
-invariances are not relevant. In all that follows, we restrict to
d>4
.

7.3
Relation of covariant and primary currents

For
d>4
, inserting the general constraint (
120
) for the divergence of
V
into eq. (
119
) implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}&=R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71463pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.3676pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma\delta}}}\left(\omega^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.74245pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.74245pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.32661pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.94757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[\gamma|\delta]}}}_{{{\nu\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[18.38002pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.38002pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.79797pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.28424pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta]}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.53888pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52776pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{\gamma\delta}_{[\nu\alpha}\theta_{\beta]}\right)\\
&=R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta}-\frac{1}{3}R^{{{\alpha\beta\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}-\frac{2}{3}G_{\nu\alpha}\theta^{\alpha}\end{split}

(148)

As guaranteed by the derivation above, this vanishes by the algebraic Bianchi identity
R_{[\alpha\beta\gamma]\delta}=0
and the field equation
R_{\mu\nu}=0
.

Note that, if we do not impose the Bianchi identity and field equations, the three contributions to eq. (
148
) are of precisely the same form as the 1-form primary currents
j[k]
,
j[\lambda]
and
j[\kappa]
defined in eqs. (
19
), (
34
) and (
46
) respectively. Indeed, if we allow sources for both the graviton and its dual then
R_{\mu\nu}\neq 0
and
R_{[\mu\nu\rho]\sigma}\neq 0

[
4
]
.
The Poincaré lemma then implies that the covariant 2-form
\Omega[V]
is related to the secondary currents
J[k]
,
J[\lambda]
and
J[\kappa]
up to the divergence of a 3-form locally.
We will see a concrete realisation of this in the following.

7.4
Sub-cases

A full solution of the constraint (
120
) for
V
will be given in section
7.5
. In order to illustrate the methods used, we first solve several simpler sub-cases. We begin with the case where only
\zeta
and
\theta
are non-zero in section
7.4.1
. Next, we consider the case where only
\omega
is non-zero in section
7.4.2
, and finally we include all three terms in the constraint in section
7.5
. As discussed above,
V
is only fixed up to the addition of a solution
V_{0}
of the homogeneous equation (
140
), which adds a derivative term
\partial^{\rho}W_{\mu\nu\rho}
to
\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}
.

7.4.1
Case 1:
\omega=0
— Penrose charges

Firstly, we study solutions to the constraint (
120
) with
\omega=0
. We will show that, up to the addition of a
\differential_{L}
-coclosed tensor, the unique solution for
V
takes the form of the solution presented in eq. (
38
), for which
\Omega[V]=Y[K]
, so that we recover the Penrose charges.

Given the role that the Penrose solution (
38
) will play, we first verify that it solves the constraint (
120
). This must be the case, since we know
Y[K]
is conserved.
With
V
given by eq. (
38
), using the CKY condition (
20
), we find

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}V^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[11.2791pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.05649pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{|\gamma\delta}}}_{{{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.07574pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33981pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}

\displaystyle=6\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\mu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\nu}\partial^{\mu}K_{\alpha\beta]}

\displaystyle=6\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\mu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\nu}\tilde{K}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+12\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\mu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\nu}\delta^{\mu}_{\alpha}\hat{K}_{\beta]}

\displaystyle=-3\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\tilde{K}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta]}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.53888pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52776pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+3(d-3)\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\alpha}\hat{K}_{\beta]}

(149)

So
V
given in eq. (
38
) is indeed a solution of the constraint (
120
) with

\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta}=0,\quad\zeta_{\mu\nu\rho}=-3\tilde{K}_{\mu\nu\rho},\quad\theta_{\mu}=3(d-3)\hat{K}_{\mu}

(150)

From the properties of CKY tensors discussed in section
2
, we see that
\zeta
is a rank-3 closed CKY tensor and
\theta
is a Killing vector in this case.

We now turn to the main goal of this sub-section, to study solutions of the constraint (
120
) with
\omega=0
. That is, we wish to find the
{[4,2]}
-tensors
V
which satisfy

\partial^{\mu}V^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[11.2791pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.05649pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{|\gamma\delta}}}_{{{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.07574pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33981pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta]}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.53888pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52776pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\alpha}\theta_{\beta]}

(151)

In terms of irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations, this is the sub-case of eq. (
120
) where, as well as the constraints of eq. (
7
), we also have

\mathcal{Y}^{SO}_{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}&\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}}}(\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V)=0

We begin by finding integrability conditions on
\zeta
and
\theta
which follow from eq. (
151
).
Taking the divergence of eq. (
151
) gives

\displaystyle 0=\partial^{\nu}\left(\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta]}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.53888pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52776pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\alpha}\theta_{\beta]}\right)

(157)

Expanding the anti-symmetrisations, we have

0=\frac{1}{6}\left(\partial_{\gamma}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta}-3\eta_{\gamma[\alpha}\partial^{\nu}\zeta_{\beta\delta]\nu}+2\eta_{\delta[\alpha|}\partial_{\gamma}\theta_{|\beta]}+\eta_{\gamma[\alpha}\eta_{\beta]\delta}\partial^{\nu}\theta_{\nu}\right)-(\gamma\leftrightarrow\delta)

(158)

Now, anti-symmetrising this equation over all the indices immediately gives

\partial_{[\gamma}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta]}=0

(159)

so
\zeta
is a closed 3-form. Furthermore, contracting eq. (
158
) with
\eta^{\alpha\gamma}
yields

0=(3-d)\partial^{\alpha}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta}+(2-d)\left(\partial_{\delta}\theta_{\beta}-\eta_{\delta\beta}\partial^{\alpha}\theta_{\alpha}\right)

(160)

and further contracting with
\eta^{\beta\delta}
gives

\partial^{\alpha}\theta_{\alpha}=0

(161)

Substituting this into eq. (
160
) and symmetrising over
\beta
and
\delta
gives

\partial_{(\delta}\theta_{\beta)}=0

(162)

so we find that
\theta
is a Killing vector. Eq. (
160
) then implies

(3-d)\partial^{\alpha}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta}=(2-d)\partial_{\beta}\theta_{\delta}

(163)

Substituting this back into eq. (
158
) and rearranging yields

\left(\partial_{\gamma}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta}-\frac{3}{d-2}\eta_{\gamma[\alpha}\partial^{\nu}\zeta_{\beta\delta]\nu}\right)-(\gamma\leftrightarrow\delta)=0

(164)

Antisymmetrising this equation over the
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\delta
indices and using eq. (
159
) then finally gives

\partial_{\alpha}\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}-\frac{3}{d-2}\eta_{\alpha[\beta}\partial^{\nu}\zeta_{\gamma\delta]\nu}=0

(165)

which is the condition for
\zeta
to be a rank-3 closed CKY tensor.
Therefore, it follows from (
151
) that
\theta
must be a Killing vector and and
\zeta
must be a
rank-3 closed CKY tensor. Furthermore, they are related non-locally by eq. (
163
).

From the discussion of section
2
, we know that
\theta
and
\zeta
can be written as the divergence and curl of a CKY 2-form respectively (see Appendix A of Ref.
[
6
]
for further details). The construction works as follows.
A general Minkowski space Killing vector and rank-3 closed CKY tensor can be parameterised by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta_{\mu}&=\alpha_{\mu}+\beta_{\mu\nu}x^{\nu}\\
\zeta_{\mu\nu\rho}&=\gamma_{\mu\nu\rho}+\tilde{\beta}_{[\mu\nu}x_{\rho]}\end{split}

(166)

where
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\tilde{\beta}
and
\gamma
are constant antisymmetric tensors. The relation between
\zeta
and
\theta
in eq. (
163
) then implies that

\tilde{\beta}_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{3}{d-3}\beta_{\mu\nu}

(167)

Now, introducing a rank-2 CKY tensor
K
as in eq. (
26
) and setting its components to

\mathcal{B}_{\mu}=-\frac{2}{d-3}\alpha_{\mu},\quad\mathcal{C}_{\mu\nu\rho}=-\frac{1}{3}\gamma_{\mu\nu\rho},\quad\mathcal{D}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{3(d-3)}\beta_{\mu\nu},

(168)

we find that
\theta
and
\zeta
defined in eq. (
166
) are related to the CKY tensor
K
precisely as in eq. (
150
). The constant
\mathcal{A}_{\mu\nu}
term in the CKY decomposition is left undetermined. Substituting the relations in eq. (
150
) into (
151
) gives

\partial^{\mu}V^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.60477pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[11.2791pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.05649pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{|\gamma\delta}}}_{{{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.03123pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.07574pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33981pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=-3\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\tilde{K}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.99707pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta]}}}_{{{\alpha\beta]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.89864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.53888pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52776pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+3(d-3)\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\nu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\alpha}\hat{K}_{\beta]}

(169)

Now, reversing the manipulations in eq. (
149
) implies

\partial^{\mu}V^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[22.80075pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[22.80075pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.64021pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[9.0287pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma\delta}}}_{{{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71463pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.3676pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\partial^{\mu}\left(6\delta^{[\gamma}_{[\mu}\delta^{\delta]}_{\nu}K_{\alpha\beta]}\right)

(170)

such that, up to the addition of a
\differential_{L}
-coclosed tensor,
V
is given by the solution presented in eq. (
38
) which yields the Penrose charges.

Let us now discuss the charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
which result from these solutions of eq. (
151
).
With
V
satisfying eq. (
170
), the divergence of
\Omega[V]
is given by

\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71463pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.3676pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma\delta}}}\partial^{\mu}\left(6\delta^{\gamma}_{[\mu}\delta^{\delta}_{\nu}K_{\alpha\beta]}\right)=\partial^{\mu}\left(R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83525pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71463pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.3676pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma\delta}}}\delta^{\gamma}_{[\mu}\delta^{\delta}_{\nu}K_{\alpha\beta]}\right)=\partial^{\mu}Y[K]_{\mu\nu}

(171)

where we have used the differential Bianchi identity
\partial_{[\mu}R_{\alpha\beta]\gamma\delta}=0
in the second equality and eq. (
39
) in the final equality.
The Poincaré lemma then implies that
\Omega[V]
is given locally by the Penrose 2-form up to the divergence of a 3-form, i.e.

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=Y[K]_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{\rho}W_{\mu\nu\rho}

(172)

Up to the addition of such a term, we see that when
\omega=0
in the constraint (
120
)
the unique solution for
\Omega[V]
is given by the Penrose 2-form
Y[K]
.

7.4.2
Case 2:
\zeta=\theta=0
—
\kappa
-type charges

We now consider the simplification of the constraint (
120
) in which
\theta=0
and
\zeta=0
, but
\omega
is non-zero.
In this case, the constraint becomes

\partial^{\mu}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta[\gamma|\delta]}

(173)

In terms of irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations, this is the sub-case of eq. (
120
) where, as well as the constraints of eq. (
7
),
\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V
also satisfies

\mathcal{Y}^{SO}_{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}}}(\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V)=\mathcal{Y}^{SO}_{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}}}(\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V)=0

A straightforward integrability condition of eq. (
173
) is found by taking a further divergence of eq. (
173
),

\partial^{\nu}\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta[\gamma|\delta]}=0

(179)

Antisymmetrising this equation on the
\beta
and
\delta
indices then implies

\partial^{\nu}\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta}=0

(180)

We note that this is the property obeyed by
\tilde{\kappa}
in eq. (
43
), which points to a relation between the covariant charges related to
\Omega[V]
and the
\kappa
-charges.
Recalling that
\omega
is also required to be traceless, it satisfies all the same properties required of a
\tilde{\kappa}
tensor (see section
3
).
We conclude that the tensor
\omega
appearing in the constraint (
173
) must be a
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensor.

Furthermore, we now show that
all

\tilde{\kappa}
-tensors can be written as in eq. (
173
) for some choice of
V
on Minkowski space.
In fact, the relevant choice for
V
which gives rise to any given
\tilde{\kappa}
tensor has already been given in eq. (
59
), which we repeat here for convenience,

V[\tilde{\kappa}]_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}\tilde{\kappa}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|[\gamma}x_{\delta]}

(181)

For this choice of
V
, it then follows from eq. (
52
) that eq. (
173
) is satisfied with

\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}\tilde{\kappa}_{\nu\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta}

(182)

Hence, the tensors
\omega
appearing in constraint (
173
) are in one-to-one correspondence with
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensors, and for each such tensor we can construct a conserved 2-form
\Omega[V]
.

We recall from the discussion in section
4.2
that the form of
V
given in eq. (
181
) is not necessarily well-defined when
\mathcal{M}
has toroidal directions due to the explicit coordinate dependence of
V
. If
\mathcal{M}
does have compact directions, it may be that there is no well-defined
V
which satisfies eq. (
173
) for a given
\tilde{\kappa}
. We have, however, seen examples is section
4.2
of
\kappa
-tensors on such spacetimes and have constructed
{[4,2]}
-tensors
V
which satisfy eq. (
52
) for these
\kappa
-tensors.

Let us now study the charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
associated with the solutions of eq. (
173
). With
V
given by eq. (
59
), these are the same covariant charges constructed in section
4
, so
\mathbb{Q}[V[\tilde{\kappa}]]=\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
. In section
4.3
, we saw that
\kappa
must represent a non-trivial
\differential_{L}
-cohomology class if the charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
is to be non-zero.

Therefore, when we impose the simplification that
\zeta=0
and
\theta=0
in the constraint (
120
), the non-trivial charges
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
are labelled by
\tilde{\kappa}
tensors which are
\differential_{L}
-coclosed but not
\differential_{L}
-coexact, and so correspond to the
\differential_{L}
-cohomology of
{\lfloor d-4,1\rfloor}
-tensors.
These charges are related to the charges
Q[\kappa]=\int\star J[\kappa]
by eq. (
56
).

7.5
The general case

We now consider the full constraint (
120
) with all components
\omega
,
\zeta
and
\theta
non-zero. We determine integrability conditions on these tensors by similar manipulations to those in the sub-cases considered in previous sections.
In section
7.4.1
, we addressed the case where
\omega=0
and found that integrability conditions impose that
\zeta
and
\theta
must be a closed CKY 3-form and a Killing vector respectively. They ultimately appeared in a combination which yielded the Penrose 2-form.
Including
\omega
will alter the manipulations somewhat, but the result is similar. We emphasise again that we are aiming to find particular solutions to eq. (
120
), to which we can always add a
\differential_{L}
-coclosed
{[4,2]}
-tensor to give another solution.

We begin as in eq. (
157
), by taking the divergence
\partial^{\nu}
of the constraint (
120
), giving

-\partial^{\nu}\omega_{\nu\alpha\beta[\gamma|\delta]}=\frac{1}{6}\left(\partial_{\gamma}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta}-3\eta_{\gamma[\alpha}\partial^{\nu}\zeta_{\beta\delta]\nu}+2\eta_{\delta[\alpha|}\partial_{\gamma}\theta_{|\beta]}+\eta_{\gamma[\alpha}\eta_{\beta]\delta}\partial^{\nu}\theta_{\nu}\right)-(\gamma\leftrightarrow\delta)

(183)

Antisymmetrising over all the free indices then implies

\partial_{[\gamma}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\delta]}=-3\partial^{\nu}\omega_{\nu[\alpha\beta\gamma|\delta]}

(184)

which is in contrast to eq. (
159
) when
\omega=0
.
Contracting eq. (
183
) with
\eta^{\alpha\gamma}
, the left-hand side vanishes as
\omega
is traceless. So eq. (
160
) is unchanged by the presence of
\omega
. Then the manipulations of eqs. (
161
) to (
163
) go through unchanged. In particular, this implies that
\theta
is a Killing vector even when
\omega\neq 0
.

Following the same manipulations which led to eq. (
165
) now gives

\partial_{\alpha}\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}-\frac{3}{d-2}\eta_{\alpha[\beta}\partial^{\nu}\zeta_{\gamma\delta]\nu}=3\partial^{\nu}\omega_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\alpha}

(185)

which recovers eq. (
165
) when
\omega=0
. Therefore, the presence of
\omega
implies that
\zeta
is no longer a rank-3 closed CKY tensor.

Contracting eq. (
185
) with
\partial^{\beta}
annihilates the right-hand side, giving

\partial_{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}=0

(186)

Therefore,

\partial^{\beta}\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}=c_{\gamma\delta}

(187)

is a constant 2-form. It then follows that
\zeta
is at most linear in the coordinates, up to the addition of a co-closed 3-form. The most general such object is then

\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}=a_{\beta\gamma\delta}+b_{\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}x^{\sigma}+\alpha_{\beta\gamma\delta}

(188)

where
\alpha
is a co-closed 3-form,
a
is a constant 3-form and
b
is a constant
{\lfloor 3,1\rfloor}
-tensor with
b^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[16.56564pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[16.56564pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.8473pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.3195pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\gamma\delta\beta|}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=c_{\gamma\delta}
to satisfy eq. (
187
). The standard way to proceed with such manipulations is to insert eq. (
188
) back into eq. (
185
) to constrain the form of
b
. However, this is less useful in the present case due to the non-zero right-hand side. Instead, a neater way forward is to decompose
b
into
SO(1,d-1)
representations,

b_{\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}=\bar{b}_{\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}+\frac{3}{d-2}b^{\prime}_{[\beta\gamma}\eta_{\delta]\sigma}

(189)

where
b^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=b^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.12967pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.12967pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.80408pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.00291pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{\alpha\beta\gamma|}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
is the trace of
b
, and
\bar{b}
is traceless.
Now, eq. (
188
) gives

\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}=a_{\beta\gamma\delta}+\frac{3}{d-2}b^{\prime}_{[\beta\gamma}x_{\delta]}+\bar{b}_{\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}x^{\sigma}+\alpha_{\beta\gamma\delta}

(190)

However, using the fact that
\bar{b}
is traceless, we note that the
\bar{b}
term can be written as the total divergence of a 4-form,

\bar{b}_{\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}x^{\sigma}=\frac{4}{d-2}\partial^{\alpha}\left(x_{[\alpha}\bar{b}_{\beta\gamma\delta]|\sigma}x^{\sigma}\right)

(191)

and so this term can be absorbed into a redefinition of
\alpha
. Therefore, the most general form of
\zeta
is

\zeta_{\beta\gamma\delta}=\zeta^{0}_{\beta\gamma\delta}+\alpha_{\beta\gamma\delta}

(192)

where
\zeta^{0}_{\beta\gamma\delta}=a_{\beta\gamma\delta}+\frac{3}{d-2}b^{\prime}_{[\beta\gamma}x_{\delta]}
, which is the general form of a rank-3 closed CKY tensor on flat space. So
\zeta
has contributions from a closed CKY 3-form and a co-closed 3-form.

Substituting eq. (
192
) into eq. (
185
) then implies

\partial^{\nu}\omega_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}=\frac{1}{3}\partial_{\sigma}\alpha_{\beta\gamma\delta}

(193)

This can be rewritten in the form

\partial^{\nu}\pi_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}=0

(194)

where

\pi_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}=\omega_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}-\frac{4}{3}\eta_{\sigma[\nu}\alpha_{\beta\gamma\delta]}

(195)

We see that, in contrast to the results of section
7.4.2
,
\omega
is no longer
\differential_{L}
-coclosed. Instead, eq. (
194
) suggests that
\pi
is the correct object to be associated with
\tilde{\kappa}
in this case. However, we see that
\pi
is not traceless:

\pi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[21.57413pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[21.57413pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[11.88998pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.49283pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}_{{{\nu\beta\gamma\sigma|}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\frac{d-3}{3}\alpha_{\nu\beta\gamma}

(196)

As mentioned in section
3
, the current
J[\kappa]
in eq. (
45
) is conserved irrespective of whether
\tilde{\kappa}
is traceless or not. When
\tilde{\kappa}
is traceless, this charge corresponds directly to an invariance of the dual graviton. Now, we are forced allow a non-zero trace for
\tilde{\kappa}
in order to associate it with
\pi
, which is
\differential_{L}
-coclosed. With this subtlety addressed, we make the association

\pi_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}=\frac{(-1)^{d}}{d-1}\tilde{\kappa}_{\nu\beta\gamma\delta|\sigma}

(197)

Then, as in section
7.4.2
, a solution for
V
which is related to this
\pi
by eq. (
52
) is given by
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
in eq. (
181
). Again, if
\tilde{\kappa}
is
\differential_{L}
-coexact then the associated charge
\mathbb{Q}[\kappa]
vanishes and non-trivial charges associated with
\tilde{\kappa}
correspond to the
\differential_{L}
-cohomology of
{\lfloor d-4,1\rfloor}
-tensors.
As noted in section
4
, we point out that the
V
given in eq. (
59
) is not well-defined when
\mathcal{M}
has compact dimensions and in that case it is not guaranteed that all
\tilde{\kappa}
tensors have a corresponding
V[\tilde{\kappa}]
.

Therefore, we find that including all three terms in the constraint (
120
) does not greatly change the conclusions reached in the previous two sections. Namely,
\theta
must be a Killing vector,
\zeta
comprises a rank-3 closed CKY tensor and a co-closed 3-form
\alpha
which combines with
\omega
in such a way that the
{\lfloor 4,1\rfloor}
-tensor
\pi
is
\differential_{L}
-coclosed and so must be a
\tilde{\kappa}
tensor (although now it is not traceless).

The covariant charges of
\Omega[V]
associated with these tensors are then related to the secondary currents
J[\kappa]
,
J[\lambda]
and
J[k]
as follows. By analogous manipulations to eq. (
53
), up to the addition of a coclosed 2-form, we have

\displaystyle\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma^{\gamma\delta|\beta}\partial^{\alpha}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}+\partial^{\alpha}\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma^{\gamma\delta|\beta}\left(\omega_{\mu\nu\beta\gamma|\delta}+\eta_{\gamma[\mu}\zeta_{\nu\beta]\delta}+\eta_{\gamma[\mu}\theta_{\beta}\eta_{\nu]\delta}\right)+\partial^{\alpha}\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma^{\gamma\delta|\beta}\left(\omega_{\mu\nu\beta\gamma|\delta}+\eta_{\gamma[\mu}\alpha_{\nu\beta]\delta}+\eta_{\gamma[\mu}\zeta^{0}_{\nu\beta]\delta}+\eta_{\gamma[\mu}\theta_{\beta}\eta_{\nu]\delta}\right)+\partial^{\alpha}\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}

(198)

where
\Phi
is the 3-form given in eq. (
54
). We have used the constraint (
120
) in the second equality and eq. (
192
) in the third. Now, writing the Killing vector
\theta
as the divergence of a CKY 2-form
K
and writing the closed CKY 3-form
\zeta^{0}
as its curl, the final two terms of the previous equation rearrange into the Penrose current as explained in section
7.4.1
.
The first two terms in the previous equation can also be straightforwardly rearranged, yielding

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=J[\kappa]_{\mu\nu}+Y[K]_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{\alpha}\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}

(199)

where
\zeta^{0}
and
\theta
are given in terms of
K
by eq. (
150
) and
\pi
is related to
\tilde{\kappa}
by eq. (
197
). Then from eq. (
35
), we have

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=J[k]_{\mu\nu}+J[\lambda]_{\mu\nu}+J[\kappa]_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{\alpha}(\Phi_{\mu\nu\alpha}+Z[K]_{\mu\nu\alpha})

(200)

with
k
and
\tilde{\lambda}
given by eqs. (
28
) and (
29
) respectively (they are proportional to
\theta
and
\zeta
). We recall from Refs.
[
12
,
6
]
that the Penrose 2-form
Y[K]
is co-exact in
d>4
when
K
is a KY tensor. So non-trivial charges result only from the
\mathcal{B}
- and
\mathcal{D}
-type CKY tensors in the
Y[K]
contribution to eq. (
199
).

In summary, in this section we have studied solutions to the general constraint on
V
in eq. (
120
). We have looked for particular solutions to this equation, to which one can always add a
{[4,2]}
-tensor which satisfies eq. (
140
). Such extra terms can contribute to the charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
on spaces with non-trivial cohomology (see section
7.1
). We then analysed the conserved 2-forms
\Omega[V]
associated with the solutions of eq. (
120
), and their relation to the secondary currents
J[k]
,
J[\lambda]
and
J[\kappa]
. One can always add a coclosed 2-form to
\Omega[V]
without spoiling conservation. If this 2-form is locally the divergence of a non-covariant 3-form, it can produce non-trivial charges. Since the addition of such a term is arbitrary, we only classify the charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
up to the addition of such terms.

8
Dimensional reduction of charges in Kaluza-Klein theory

In the previous section we have given a general discussion of the gauge-invariant 2-form currents which are conserved in the linearised graviton theory in
d
dimensions. We now consider a toroidal compactification of this theory on
T^{n}
and ask whether the conserved quantities in
D\equiv d-n
dimensions can be related to those in
d
dimensions.

Concretely, we consider
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{1,D-1}\times T^{n}
and denote the coordinates
x^{\mu}=(x^{m},y^{i})
where
y^{i}\sim y^{i}+2\pi R_{i}
. The metric on
\mathcal{M}
is the Minkowski metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
.

8.1
Kaluza-Klein Ansatz

We will focus in particular on compactifications where the graviton components are independent of the compact directions:

\partial_{i}h_{\mu\nu}=0

(201)

and take an Ansatz of the form

h_{\mu\nu}\quad\longrightarrow\quad h_{mn}=\bar{h}_{mn}-\frac{2}{D-2}\eta_{mn}\phi,\quad h_{mi}=2A_{m}^{(i)},\quad h_{ij}=2\phi^{(ij)}

(202)

where
\phi\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{(ii)}
. We will refer to
\bar{h}_{mn}
as the
D
-dimensional graviton. There are also
n
one-form fields
A^{(i)}_{m}
which are the graviphotons and
n(n+1)/2
scalars
\phi^{(ij)}
sitting in a symmetric matrix.
Eq. (
201
) implies

\partial_{i}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=0

(203)

The components of the
d
-dimensional linearised Riemann tensor can be written

\begin{split}R_{mnpq}&=\bar{R}_{mnpq}-\frac{2}{D-2}\left(\eta_{m[p}\partial_{q]}\partial_{n}\phi-\eta_{n[p}\partial_{q]}\partial_{m}\phi\right)\\
R_{mnpi}&=\partial_{p}F^{(i)}_{mn}\\
R_{minj}&=\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\phi^{(ij)}\end{split}

(204)

where
\bar{R}_{mnpq}=-2\partial_{[m}\bar{h}_{n][p,q]}
is the
D
-dimensional linearised Riemann tensor and
F^{(i)}_{mn}=\partial_{m}A^{(i)}_{n}-\partial_{n}A^{(i)}_{m}
is the field strength of the
i^{\text{th}}
graviphoton.

Here we will consider the theory in the absence of sources, in which case the
d
-dimensional equations of motion imply that the
D
-dimensional fields obey

\begin{split}\bar{G}_{mn}&=0\\
\partial^{p}F^{(i)}_{pm}&=0\\
\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\phi^{(ij)}&=0\end{split}

(205)

See Ref.
[
6
]
for the a discussion of these equations in the presence of sources.

8.2
2-form currents in the
D
-dimensional theory

In the dimensionally reduced theory, there are several 2-form currents which can be constructed from the fields
\bar{h}_{mn}
,
A^{(i)}_{m}
and
\phi^{(ij)}
. Firstly, we construct the
D
-dimensional 2-forms

\bar{\Omega}[\bar{V}]_{mn}=\bar{R}^{abcd}\bar{V}_{mnab|cd}

(206)

where
\bar{V}
is a
{[4,2]}
-tensor in
D
-dimensions. The considerations of section
7
then apply and
\bar{V}
must satisfy

\partial^{m}\bar{V}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.89375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.89375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[11.9364pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[8.52599pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{|cd}}}_{{{mnab}\mathchoice{\makebox[11.9326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.9326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.032pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30855pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}=\bar{\omega}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.79764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.79764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63414pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45294pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[c|d]}}}_{{{nab}\mathchoice{\makebox[18.28139pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.28139pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.75423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25298pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{[c}_{[n}\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.11821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.11821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.05411pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32434pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d]}}}_{{{ab]}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42677pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42677pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.9115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79391pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+\delta^{cd}_{[na}\theta_{b]}

(207)

where,
\bar{\omega}
is a traceless
{[4,1]}
-tensor,
\zeta
is a 3-form and
\theta
is a 1-form. Consistency then implies that
\theta
is a Killing vector, and
\bar{\omega}
and
\zeta
assemble into a rank-3 closed CKY tensor and a
\tilde{\kappa}
-tensor as in section
7.5
.

In a similar manner, we can construct conserved 2-forms involving the graviphotons. To do so, we contract the
R^{abci}
component of the
d
-dimensional Riemann tensor (
204
) with some object. This ensures the result will be gauge-invariant when we uplift to
d
dimensions.

There are, in fact, multiple ways to do this but it is sufficient to consider a 2-form of the form

\Delta[\chi]_{mn}=R^{abci}\chi^{(i)}_{mnc|ab}=\partial^{c}F^{(i)ab}\chi^{(i)}_{mnc|ab}

(208)

for some
{[3,2]}
-tensors
\chi^{(i)}
.
We could also consider a 2-form constructed like
R^{abci}\xi^{(i)}_{mnab|c}
for some
{[4,1]}
-tensors
\xi^{(i)}
, however we find that these merely produce a subset of the charges of those produced by the
\Delta[\chi]_{mn}
above.

We must require that
\Delta[\chi]
is conserved. This will be the case provided that the
\chi^{(i)}
obey a differential condition. This can be derived analogously to the manipulations in Appendix
C
as follows.
\partial^{m}\chi^{(i)}_{mnc|ab}
is, a priori, in the
{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}}\otimes{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}}
representation of
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
. This can be decomposed into irreducible representations of
SO(1,d-1)
. Demanding conservation of
\Delta[\chi]
then implies that only some of these irreducible representations can appear. In this case, the allowed
SO(1,d-1)
representations are

and
\bullet
. Therefore, we demand that
\chi^{(i)}
satisfies

\partial^{m}\chi^{(i)}_{mnc|ab}=\gamma^{(i)}_{ncab}+(\eta_{a[n}\eta_{c]b}\delta^{(i)}-a\leftrightarrow b)

(217)

where
\gamma^{(i)}=\mathcal{Y}^{SO}_{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to2.71053pt{\vss\hbox to2.3917pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.71053pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\vrule height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}}}(\differential_{L}^{\dagger}\chi^{(i)})
are 4-forms and
\delta^{(i)}=\mathcal{Y}^{SO}_{\bullet}(\differential_{L}^{\dagger}\chi^{(i)})
are 0-forms. Contracting the
c
and
b
indices and then taking the divergence of this equation implies
\partial_{a}\delta^{(i)}=0
, so
\delta^{(i)}
are constants. Given this, taking a divergence of eq. (
217
) implies that the
\gamma^{(i)}
are coclosed.

We can also construct conserved 2-forms from the scalar fields
\phi^{(ij)}
. The components of the Riemann tensor involving the scalars is given in the final line of eq. (
204
). The most general conserved 2-form that can be built involving this gauge-invariant combination of derivatives of
\phi^{(ij)}
is of the form

\Psi[\mu]_{mn}=\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\phi^{(ij)}\mu^{(ij)}_{mna|b}

(222)

where
\mu^{(ij)}
is a
{[3,1]}
-tensor for each
i,j
. Similar arguments to above show that conservation of this 2-form implies that
\mu^{(ij)}
must satisfy

\partial^{m}\mu^{(ij)}_{mna|b}=\nu^{(ij)}_{nab}

(223)

where
\nu^{(ij)}
is a 3-form for each
i,j
, from which it follows that
\nu^{(ij)}
is coclosed.

Therefore, we have conserved 2-forms
\bar{\Omega}[\bar{V}]_{mn}
in (
206
) where
\bar{V}
satisfies (
207
),
\Delta[\chi]_{mn}
in (
208
) where
\chi^{(i)}
satisfies (
217
), and
\Psi[\mu]_{mn}
in (
222
) where
\mu^{(ij)}
satisfies (
223
).

Charges in the
D
-dimensional theory

Let us now consider the charges associated with the conserved 2-forms
\bar{\Omega}[\bar{V}]_{mn}
,
\Sigma[\xi]_{mn}
and
\Psi[\mu]_{mn}
constructed above. The construction of
\bar{\Omega}[\bar{V}]
is exactly analogous to that of
\Omega[V]
in section
7
, just in
D
dimensions. Therefore, there are associated charges

\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\bar{V}]=\int_{\Sigma_{D-2}}\star\bar{\Omega}[\bar{V}]

(224)

where
\Sigma_{D-2}
is a codimension-2 cycle in
D
-dimensions.
The discussion of the
d
-dimensional charges
\mathbb{Q}[V]
in section
7.5
carries through analogously here.

Next we consider the charges constructed from the conserved 2-forms
\Delta[\chi]
. From eq. (
217
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta[\chi]_{mn}&=\partial^{c}(F^{(i)ab}\chi^{(i)}_{mnc|ab})-F^{(i)ab}(\gamma^{(i)}_{mnab}+2\eta_{a[m}\eta_{n]b}\delta^{(i)})\\
&=\partial^{c}(F^{(i)ab}\chi^{(i)}_{mnc|ab})-F^{(i)ab}\gamma^{(i)}_{mnab}-\delta^{(i)}F^{(i)}_{mn}\end{split}

(225)

The first term is the divergence of a globally defined 3-form and so will vanish by Stokes’ theorem when integrated over a cycle. Therefore, the related charge is

\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\chi]\equiv\int_{\Sigma_{D-2}}\star\Delta[\chi]=-2\int_{\Sigma_{D-2}}\star\gamma^{(i)}\wedge F^{(i)}-\delta^{(i)}\int_{\Sigma_{D-2}}\star F^{(i)}

(226)

The first term on the right-hand side is a magnetic charge which depends only on the cohomology class of the closed
(D-4)
-form
\star\gamma^{(i)}
. It is carried by
(D-4)
-branes wrapping cycles which are Poincaré dual to the cohomology class
[\star\gamma^{(i)}]

[
2
]
. The second term on the right hand side is the electric charge of the graviphotons, which generates the standard electric
U(1)
1-form symmetry of Maxwell theory
[
27
]
.

Finally we consider the charges which are constructed from the 2-forms
\Psi[\mu]
, related to the scalars
\phi^{(ij)}
. The charge is

\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\mu]=\int_{\Sigma_{D-2}}\star\Psi[\mu]

(227)

From the definition (
222
), stripping off derivatives and using eq. (
223
) yields

\Psi[\mu]_{mn}=\partial^{a}\left(\partial^{b}\phi^{(ij)}\mu^{(ij)}_{mna|b}-\phi^{(ij)}\nu^{(ij)}_{mna}\right)

(228)

The first term inside the parentheses is globally defined since the gauge transformations of the scalars
\phi^{(ij)}
are simply shifts by a constant. Therefore, this term does not contribute to the charge. The charge can then be written

\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\mu]=-\int_{\Sigma_{D-2}}\differential\star(\phi^{(ij)}\nu^{(ij)})

(229)

which is a topological charge and can be non-zero only when
\phi^{(ij)}
are not globally defined. Physically, this measures the winding of the scalars around the compact directions.

8.3
Embedding in
d
-dimensional theory

Having constructed the
D
-dimensional charges which can be constructed from the fields
\bar{h}
,
A^{(i)}
, and
\phi^{(ij)}
, we now wish to embed them into the
d
-dimensional charges
\Omega[V]
which can be constructed from the
d
-dimensional graviton
h
.
Firstly, we define a
{[4,2]}
-tensor
V
in
d
-dimensions with components

V_{mnab|cd}=\bar{V}_{mnab|cd},\quad V_{mnai|cd}=V_{mncd|ai}=\frac{1}{4}\chi^{(i)}_{mna|cd},\quad V_{mnai|bj}=\frac{1}{4}\mu^{(ij)}_{mna|b}

(230)

and a
d
-dimensional
{[2,2]}
-tensor
\mathscr{P}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
with components

\mathscr{P}_{mnpq}=\frac{2}{D-2}\left(\eta_{m[p}\partial_{q]}\partial_{n}\phi-m\leftrightarrow n\right)

(231)

and all other components vanishing. Now consider the object

\Omega[V]_{mn}=\bar{\Omega}[\bar{V}]_{mn}+\Delta[\chi]_{mn}+\Psi[\mu]_{mn}

(232)

This is the
mn
component of the tensor

\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=(R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}+\mathscr{P}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta})V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}

(233)

This can be seen by expanding

\displaystyle\Omega[V]_{mn}

\displaystyle=(R^{abcd}+\mathscr{P}^{abcd})V_{mnab|cd}+4R^{abci}V_{mnab|ci}+4R^{aibj}V_{mnai|bj}

(234)

and substituting in eqs. (
204
) and comparing with the definitions (
206
), (
208
) and (
222
).

Therefore, if we choose to integrate
\star\Omega[V]
over a
(d-2)
-cycle given by

\Sigma_{d-2}=\Sigma_{D-2}\times T^{n}

(235)

then

\mathbb{Q}[V]=\int_{\Sigma_{d-2}}\star\Omega[V]=\text{Vol}_{T^{n}}\left(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\bar{V}]+\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\chi]+\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\mu]\right)

(236)

Therefore, we see that the gauge-invariant charges
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\bar{V}]
,
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\chi]
, and
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}[\mu]
which are constructed in the dimensionally reduced
D
-dimensional theory have a unified gauge-invariant origin in the original
d
-dimensional theory.

9
Discussion & outlook

In this work we have focused on the construction of gauge-invariant 2-form conserved currents in the theory of the linearised graviton on a background spacetime with a flat metric but which may have non-trivial topology due to toroidal compactification or the removal of some regions.
These currents can then be used to define charges which are codimension-2 topological operators. In the quantum theory, these generate 1-form symmetries.
In particular, we have found a covariant expression for the gravitational magnetic charge of
[
2
]
constructed from a
[d-4,1]
Killing tensor
\kappa
which satisfies the differential constraint (
33
).
This charge is interesting as it is carried by the linearised Kaluza-Klein monopole solution.

Furthermore, we have classified all such 2-form currents of the general form (
2
). We find that, besides the
\kappa
-currents above, the other non-trivial conserved 2-forms are the Penrose currents (
1
) which have been discussed previously in
[
5
,
9
,
12
,
6
]
.

Our discussion here is purely for the linearised theory. It is of course important and interesting to consider the extension of our results to the non-linear theory of general relativity or supergravity. Kaluza-Klein monopoles play a key role in the non-linear theory and carry the Kaluza-Klein monopole charge of
[
8
]
. Extending our work to the non-linear case should shed some light onto the significance of this and related charges.
We will discuss this and related issues elsewhere.

There is a general expectation that generalised symmetries should come in dual pairs
[
28
,
12
]
. That is, there should be the same number of continuous
p
-form symmetries as
(d-p-1)
-form symmetries. This was verified for the free graviton on
d
-dimensional Minkowski space in
[
12
]
by showing that some of the Penrose charges are trivial in
d>4
and showing that there is the same number of conserved
(d-2)
-form currents as of non-trivial Penrose charges. We have shown here that, on topologically non-trivial backgrounds, there are more 1-form symmetries than those generated by the Penrose charges. It would, therefore, be interesting to understand the full set of generalised symmetries that are dual to these on such backgrounds and to see whether they come in dual pairs; we will return to this elsewhere.

Appendix A
Some definitions and conventions

The secondary current
J[k]
introduced in eq. (
18
) can be written

J[k]^{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\sigma}\mathcal{K}^{\mu\nu|\rho\sigma}k_{\rho}-\mathcal{K}^{\mu\sigma|\rho\nu}\partial_{\sigma}k_{\rho}

(237)

where

\mathcal{K}^{\mu\nu|\rho\sigma}=-3\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha|\rho\sigma\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}

(238)

and

\eta^{\mu\nu\rho|\alpha\beta\gamma}=\eta^{\alpha\sigma}\eta^{\beta\kappa}\eta^{\gamma\lambda}\delta^{\mu}_{[\sigma}\delta^{\nu}_{\kappa}\delta^{\rho}_{\lambda]}

(239)

The secondary current
J[\lambda]
in eq. (
34
), corresponding to the
\lambda
-invariances, can be explicitly constructed in terms of the dual graviton
D
and, when
\tilde{\lambda}
is constant, can be rewritten in terms of the graviton
h_{\mu\nu}
. In this case, the secondary current is

J[\lambda]_{\mu\nu}=\partial^{\rho}Z[\lambda]_{\mu\nu\rho}

(240)

where

Z[\lambda]_{\mu\nu\rho}=(-1)^{d+1}\frac{4}{d-1}\tilde{\lambda}_{[\sigma\mu\nu}h_{\rho]}{}^{\sigma}

(241)

When
\tilde{\lambda}
is not constant, there is another contribution to
Z[\lambda]
in the dual theory which cannot be written locally in terms of the graviton
h_{\mu\nu}
. In any case, the current
J[\lambda]
in eq. (
240
) is clearly conserved for any 3-form
\tilde{\lambda}
so, in the graviton theory, we will take this as the definition of
J[\lambda]
for all
\tilde{\lambda}
.

In
d=4
, the
\lambda
tensors reduce to Killing vectors (since a rank-1 KY tensor is simply a Killing vector). The secondary current
\tilde{J}[\tilde{k}]
which appears in eq. (
37
), associated to the dual graviton’s invariances in four dimensions, can be written

\tilde{J}[\tilde{k}]_{\mu\nu}=\partial^{\rho}Z[\tilde{k}]_{\mu\nu\rho}

(242)

where

Z[\tilde{k}]_{\mu\nu\rho}=-\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\tilde{k}^{\tau}h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.68787pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14204pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}_{{{\tau}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}

(243)

Appendix B
Decomposition of
V
into
\text{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
representations

As discussed in section
7
, the most general form that the tensor
V
in eq. (
50
) can take is that of a reducible
{\lfloor 2,2,2\rfloor}
-tensor, satisfying eq. (
114
). That is, it transforms in a
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representation characterised by a tensor product Young tableau

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}

We wish to impose constraints on
V
such that
\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=0
. From eq. (
116
), we have

\partial^{\mu}\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=(\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta})V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}+R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}\partial^{\mu}V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0

(251)

This must vanish for all field configurations satisfying the field equation
R_{\mu\nu}=0
and the Bianchi identities

R_{[\mu\nu\rho]\sigma}=0,\quad\partial_{[\alpha}R_{\beta\gamma]\rho\sigma}=0,\quad\partial^{\mu}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=0

(252)

The last of these equations follows from the second by using
R_{\mu\nu}=0
.
The fact that this must vanish for all such configurations implies that the two terms in eq. (
251
) must vanish independently.
The second term in eq. (
251
) will vanish provided
V
satisfies some differential condition which will be determined later (the details of this calculation can be found in Appendix
C
).
The vanishing of the first term, however, places a constraint on the symmetries of
V
(i.e. the representation which
V
transforms in) such that it projects
\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}
onto the field equations and/or Bianchi identities in such a way that the result vanishes. We determine this constraint in this Appendix, proceeding in three stages as follows.

1.

Firstly, since we are only interested in tensors
V
for which
\Omega[V]
is non-zero, we will decompose the naïve tensor product representation (
B
) which
V
transforms in into irreducible
GL(d,\mathbb{R}
) representations. Then, by contracting with the Riemann tensor to form
\Omega[V]_{\mu\nu}=R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}
we will determine which of these can contribute to
V
in a way which gives a non-trivial
\Omega[V]
from which charges can be constructed.

2.

Having found the allowed irreducible
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representations which
V
can transform in, we will insert these into eq. (
251
) and demand that the first term vanishes (the second term will be considered in Appendix
C
). Some of the representations which
V
can transform in immediately project
\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}
onto a Bianchi identity, and so vanish. For example, this is the case if
V
transforms in the irreducible [4,2] representation, then
\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\partial^{[\mu}R^{\alpha\beta]|\gamma\delta}V_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=0
from the second Bianchi identity in eq. (
252
). However, the first term of eq. (
251
) could also vanish by the third identity in eq. (
252
) if
V
has a factor of the Minkowski metric, i.e. is a sum of terms each of which is the tensor product of the Minkowski metric with another tensor. For example, if
V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=\eta_{\alpha[\mu}A_{\nu]|\beta|\gamma\delta}-(\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta)
for some
{\lfloor 1,1,2\rfloor}
-tensor
A
, then
\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}V_{\mu\nu|\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}=2\partial_{\alpha}R^{\alpha\beta|\gamma\delta}A_{\nu|\beta|\gamma\delta}=0
.

A systematic method to analyse all the ways in which the Minkowski metric can arise in
V
is to decompose the irreducible
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representations into irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations by separating out all possible traces within
V
. We can then find all the
SO(1,d-1)
representations that can contribute to
V
in a manner such that the first term in eq. (
251
) vanishes. Indeed, this is precisely the same logic employed in section
2
to show that
K
must be a CKY in order for the Penrose 2-form
Y[K]
to be conserved.

3.

Since we are ultimately interested in the set of conserved 2-forms
\Omega[V]
, rather than the tensors
V
themselves, we will ask whether all the allowed representations which
V
can transform in produce distinct 2-forms
\Omega[V]
. We will see that, while there are other representations which are allowed in the decomposition of
V
, the form of
\Omega[V]
which arises can always be constructed with a
V
in the [4,2]
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representation. Therefore, it will be sufficient to simply demand that
V
is a [4,2]-tensor.

GL(d,\mathbb{R})
decomposition of
V
.

We begin with the first stage outlined above.
Decomposing the tensor product representation in eq. (
B
) into irreducible
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representations gives

{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to3.39996pt{\vss\hbox to3.00003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-3.39996pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\vrule height=3.39996pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-3.39996pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 3.00003pt\hrule width=3.7999pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}}\otimes{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower The

contribution projects onto the differential Bianchi identity
\partial^{[\mu}R^{\alpha\beta]\gamma\delta}=0
and so vanishes. The other two terms, however, do not vanish in this manner. Instead, we should make use of the field equation
R_{\mu\nu}=0
, as well as the Bianchi identities in eq. (
252
). In order to allow for the use of the field equation, we view the irreducible
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representations as reducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations. They are reducible because
SO(1,d-1)
has the additional invariant tensor
\eta_{\mu\nu}
.
We can decompose the

and

GL(d,\mathbb{R}
) representations into
SO(1,d-1)
representations by removing all possible traces. This introduces various factors of the
SO(1,d-1)
-invariant metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
which can contract with the Riemann tensor to give the Ricci tensor.

representation, we have a decomposition

(560)

where
\bullet
represents the trivial
\mathbf{1}
representation. The degeneracies of the latter terms come from the fact that there are multiple ways to remove a trace from a tableau with more than two columns. Recall that the irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations are traceless. The decomposition of

height=2.71053pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-2.71053pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 2.3917pt\hrule width=3.02936pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}}}_{\nu\alpha]|\beta\gamma}

The goal of the decomposition into irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations was to allow for the first term of eq. (
251
) to vanish by the field equation
R_{\mu\nu}=0
. However, when we contract eq. (
B
) with
\partial^{\mu}R^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
, none of the terms vanish by using
R_{\mu\nu}=0
or the Bianchi identities in eq. (
252
). Therefore, these representations cannot be allowed in
V
if we want
\Omega[V]
to be conserved. Therefore, the irreducible
GL(d,\mathbb{R})
representation

does not give rise to any non-zero conserved 2-forms
\Omega[V]
.

We now proceed to the

GL(d)
representation in eq. (
B
), which decomposes into irreducible
SO(1,d-1)
representations as

is invariant under permutations of the three columns. Therefore, it is equivalent to take a trace between any two columns of this representation. This is why there are no representations in the decomposition (

\begin{split}{W^{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower As emphasised throughout, our intention is to classify the conserved 2-forms
\Omega[V]

Therefore, for our purposes it is sufficient to restrict

to transform in this representation. As mentioned at the beginning of this appendix, it remains to ensure that the second term in eq. (

) vanishes in order for
\Omega[V]
to be conserved. This puts a differential condition on
V
. This is considered in the main text, and the details can be found in Appendix
C

Appendix C

V
from representation theory

{[4,2]}

This vanishes for all field configurations
h_{\mu\nu}
only if
C=D=0
. That is, these representations cannot appear in
\differential^{\dagger}_{L}V
if
\Omega[V]
is to be conserved.
Therefore, the allowed
SO(1,d-1)
representations in the decomposition of
\differential_{L}^{\dagger}V
are
